- **Narration**: `⎵` plays and pauses the voice; `V` opens the panel: **off**, track, live voice, engine, tone, character, recording, captions, speed (persisted per deck). **The voice ends with the talk**: narration that runs past the last slide turns itself off and says so (`🔚 end of the talk — narration off`), instead of staying "on" in silence; and `P`/`⎵` where the live voice has nothing left to say (the last build of the last slide, its notes all spoken on earlier builds) says that (`nothing left to say — this is the end of the talk · ← goes back, Home starts over`) rather than switching the voice on into silence. **Narration is OFF until somebody picks**, however much audio the deck ships: a deck is opened to be read far more often than to be played, and defaulting to the first track made "chosen" indistinguishable from "shipped", the distinction every other decision here needs. `⎵` belongs to the voice only once a track is CHOSEN (in the panel, or restored from a previous visit, both deliberate) or the voice is already running or parked; a deck that merely carries a track keeps `⎵` for advancing, so a presenter talking over their own slides cannot start the narration by pressing it. The panel's first row is `🔇 Off`, current when nothing is chosen, and picking it stops the voice and hands `⎵` back. The choice is written as `off` rather than left implied, so a later change to what "no choice" means cannot start a deck talking that somebody switched off. Two entry points are themselves the choosing and select a track rather than refusing: `?voiceover`, where the url is the request, and the touch sound button, where a speaker icon has one meaning and the phone tapping it has no `V` key to be redirected to; neither persists, being a request for this visit rather than every future one. Two sources. **Recorded**: pre-rendered per-slide audio (`<dir>/slide-NN.m4a`, 1-based like `state.slide`), synced to slide changes; the deck configures `narration: { files: '<dir>' }` or `[{ label, dir }, …]` for several takes. **A recorded track can pace the builds, not just the slides.** `tools/voiceover.mjs` already writes one file per `⟨CLICK⟩` segment (`<dir>/slide-NN-KK.m4a`, KK 1-based over the segments that produced audio: empty segments produce none, so the file number is not the step number) and lists them as `slides[i].segments`; a track carrying them reveals build step k when segment k ends, which is the build-synced pacing the live voice has, from a recording. On a **directory** track it is opt-in: `narration: { files: '<dir>', segments: true }`, off again with `segments: false`, because the runtime cannot see the folder and must not guess. A **manifest** track needs no flag, the manifest being the list of what exists; except on a *signed* one, where a segment counts only if it carries a `url` of its own. `publish-voices.mjs` writes those urls (every beat is uploaded and signed alongside its slide) and the rule stands anyway, because a manifest published before it did lists beats whose bytes never left the author's disk, and a signature is the only thing that tells the two apart. Whether a track paces itself is settled **once**, when narration starts, from what the track declares, never by probing for a file, which neither `file://` nor a CORS-less bucket can answer. A slide recorded whole inside such a track still plays as one file and then advances; a surplus `⟨CLICK⟩` beyond the slide's builds is chained onto the last step exactly as the live voice chains it. A missing FIRST segment falls back to the whole-slide file; one missing mid-slide has nothing to fall back to, so the deck stops and names it. The voice is the clock. The character keeps up: `tools/lipsync.mjs` cuts a sidecar per beat too (`slide-NN-KK.visemes.json`, from that beat's own `slide-NN-KK.txt` as the dialog hint), the synthesized recorder writes them beside its beat WAVs, and `decklight bundle` inlines them under the same stem. A beat with no sidecar animates from amplitude rather than replaying the slide's timeline, which would start at zero against every beat and be confidently wrong. The talking-head *video* stays per slide: a clip is minutes of GPU time and the element mirrors the audio's transport rather than seeking a timeline, so cutting it per beat would cost a great deal to fix a smaller error. **Both recorders write those beats.** the panel's `Record this deck…` stitches them from the live voice's own sentence cache alongside the whole-slide file it always wrote (so a synthesized track gains build-pacing for free, with no second pass) and writes the folder's `manifest.json` after every slide (engine, model, voice, style, one entry per slide with the hash of the notes it was voiced from and its beat files, the shape `decklight voiceover` writes, hashed by the same `tools/narration-manifest.mjs` from the deck file's notes, so an aborted take is a valid partial, `decklight video` and the export dialog take the folder as a track, and re-recording the same engine and voice refreshes that folder instead of minting `voices/<voice>-2`, #535). **Its done card says what the take cost** (#565): `N clips · R reused · S sent to <engine> · ~$X`, counted for THAT run and not the session: a clip already in the window (played, or warmed by the lookahead) and one the bridge replayed from its own disk cache (`x-tts-cached: 1`) are both *reused*, so `reused + sent` is every clip the take voiced, and the spend is what the bridge priced the sent ones at, `$0` on a free or offline engine rather than blank. Re-recording an edited slide into an existing track is the case it answers: nearly everything reused, and a bill only for what changed. The your-voice recorder voices nothing, so it shows no such line, and **the panel's `Record this deck…` records them in your own voice**, for which `decklight record <deck.html>` is the door: a browser will not open a microphone for a `file://` page (getUserMedia needs a secure context, and `http://127.0.0.1` is one), so the command serves the deck and opens it at `?record`. Your-voice recorder on a `file://` deck says exactly that instead of failing silently. The deck is the teleprompter: it shows one ⟨CLICK⟩ beat at a time at the build that beat plays over, and `→` ends the take *and* reveals the next build, one gesture, so the timing you record is the timing you will present. `⌫` retakes the beat just read, `Esc` stops (every slide already finished is already written). Capture is WebAudio → 16-bit PCM → `stitchWav`, at 24 kHz mono like every engine's output: not MediaRecorder, whose webm/opus nothing else here reads, and not an AudioWorklet, whose module would have to come from a `blob:` URL that the no-trust mode's `script-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'` forbids. Each slide is written twice by EITHER recorder: one `slide-NN-KK.wav` per beat, and the whole-slide `slide-NN.wav`, so `lipsync.mjs`, `video.mjs` and a deck that never opts in all keep working: the layout on disk says nothing about which recorder made it. A beat file carries neither the segment-sized silence that separates beats (it belongs between them, not at the head of one) nor the slide's trailing `data-narration-pause`, and neither does the **whole-slide file**, nor the viseme timeline beside it: `advanceFrom` honours every pause at play time for recorded and live tracks alike, so baking one into a file as well would hold twice (which the whole-slide file quietly did until the pause gained a non-zero default and the double showed). A slide the tool would not have segmented (fewer than two beats with words in them) is one take and no segment files, the same threshold `tools/voiceover.mjs` gives up at; a slide with builds but no ⟨CLICK⟩ is recorded whole and says so on the card, because that is an authoring gap and the moment you are reading its notes aloud is the moment to learn about it. Files are written through the edit server (`POST /deck/edit/narration/record`, which is told a folder and a beat number and builds the name itself), and fall back to the browser's download folder when there is none, **saying which of the three reasons** applies, because where the files went is not something a presenter can act on and why they went there is. Whether a server exists is **awaited, never sampled**: the probe answers asynchronously and a recorder that read it early sent whole takes to the download folder because the answer had not arrived yet, not because there was no server. A deck opened with `?record` that finds no server is the loud case: that parameter means one was started, so something else is holding the port for another deck, and it says so on the intro card, before the first beat rather than after the last. **A TRACK IS A FOLDER**, and the recorders name it after the voice: a deck carries as many tracks as you have voices (several cloned ones, the system voice, two takes of your own) and `N` is the switcher, so a single `voiceover` default meant recording a second voice erased the first with nothing said. The folder is chosen by four rules in order: `--dir`/`?dir=` (asked for out loud); a folder whose `manifest.json` names this same engine and voice (a re-record **refreshes** its take rather than accumulating `-2`, `-3`); the deck's own single configured folder (a deck that names one place has decided); else `voices/<voice>`, suffixed past anything already there. `GET /deck/edit/narration/tracks` is how the deck finds out what exists (it cannot see the filesystem, which is why `segments: true` is opt-in at all) and the proposal is shown on the card as an **editable field** before recording starts, because a proposal that cannot be corrected is a decision made somewhere else. The done card then **offers to point the deck at what it just wrote**: `POST /deck/edit/narration` **adds** the track to `narration.files` (updating in place when that folder is already listed, never duplicating a row) through the same `applyEdit` door every other edit uses; the one-string form becomes a one-element list carrying the same `ext`/`segments`, which is the shape `narrationTracks()` normalises it to anyway, so `Z` takes it back and live-reload shows it. A button rather than automatic: editing someone's deck the moment a recording ends is the wrong default even with undo behind it, and the offer is absent exactly where it could not work (no edit server → the card prints the line, as before). The call site is located by the same classifier `audit` and `upgrade` use, and its argument walked with strings skipped, because `{ title: 'Acme (Inc)' }` is a deck that did nothing wrong. A deck that builds its config elsewhere (`const cfg = {…}; Decklight.init(cfg)`) has no literal to edit: that is **refused with the reason**, never guessed at. `decklight record --slides a-b` re-records a range and leaves every other slide's files alone: recording starts at slide 1 and cannot skip, so without it a fluffed beat on slide 30 costs 29 slides of reading. A `dir` may be an absolute URL, so a **public** bucket has always served a track with no extra machinery (`dir: 'https://storage.googleapis.com/…/algenib'`: media elements load cross-origin without asking). **Manifest tracks** exist for the case a `dir` cannot express: `[{ label, manifest: 'voices/algenib/manifest.signed.json' }, …]` fetches a small JSON list once when narration starts and plays each slide's URL **verbatim**, because a presigned URL carries its signature in the query string and a query string can never be a directory prefix. The manifest is the file `tools/voiceover.mjs` already writes (`{engine, voice, style, slides: [{file, hash}]}`), extended with an optional per-slide `url` and a top-level `expires` (ISO 8601); a slide with no `url` resolves its `file` next to the manifest, so an unsigned `manifest.json` in a public bucket is a track for free, and a `null` slide (no notes, no file) is silence rather than a failure. `tools/publish-voices.mjs <dir> --bucket gs://… [--sign 7d]` uploads the files whose hash moved (the last `manifest.signed.json` is the record of what is already up there; `--all` overrides), signs every one through `gcloud storage sign-url`, never our own key handling, and writes `manifest.signed.json` to deploy beside the deck while the megabytes stay in the bucket. It refuses a `--sign` longer than **7 days**, the V4 signing scheme's own ceiling, and names the public-bucket alternative for audio that must outlive a week. Signatures expiring is one more way for the voice to be unplayable, so it behaves like all the others: the expiry is checked before the first clip, narration turns off, auto-advance stops, and the message names the track, the date, and the literal re-sign command (the manifest records the `source`, `bucket` and `signDuration` it was made with, so it is a line to re-run and not a shape to fill in). A signed file that fails to load is treated the same way: a media element cannot report an HTTP status, so a 403 for a lapsed signature is indistinguishable from any other failure, and naming the only cause worth naming beats staying silent. `N` shows a manifest track with a ☁ and the time left. `decklight bundle` **inlines the manifest** under its own path (`data-decklight-voices`), like the viseme sidecars, because `fetch` is dead on `file://`; the audio stays in the bucket, which is the whole point. One honest caveat: the character overlay's `amplitude` fallback reads the audio through WebAudio, and cross-origin media taints that unless the bucket sends CORS headers. Viseme sidecars are same-origin JSON and are unaffected. **Live voice**: the player synthesizes each slide's notes on the fly through the local bridge (`decklight tts`; the deck asks its own origin for `/tts`, which `open` proxies to the bridge it started, a deck never spells a port, and falls back to `http://127.0.0.1:8787/tts` when opened from disk; override via `narration.liveUrl`). The bridge speaks with one engine at a time, six built in (`--engine`, tools/tts-engines.mjs), plus any installed one, and the deck can change which: **gemini** (gemini-2.5-{pro,flash}-tts on Vertex AI: a delivery style always reaches it; no free tier, and a fresh project's per-minute quota 429s under the lookahead's bursts), **chirp** (Chirp 3: HD on the Cloud Text-to-Speech API; the same 30 star-named voices, ~1s a sentence, 1M characters a month free, no style channel), **piper** (a local neural model: offline, unlimited, free, no credentials; one voice per installed model, and the process is held RESIDENT because piper reloads its ~120 MB model on every start), or **elevenlabs** (your own ElevenLabs account's voices, the ones you *cloned* included, which is the reason it exists). The first three know their roster up front; ElevenLabs cannot, because the interesting voices are yours, so it fetches the account's list once and `/tts/ping` and `/tts/voices` await it: **your** voices first (cloned, then professional, then premade), each tagged with where it came from, and the picker's default is the first of them. The player sends a voice NAME; a `voice_id` pasted from the dashboard resolves too. Its key is read from `$ELEVENLABS_API_KEY` and is **never written to `tts.json`**; decklight remembers the choice and leaves the secret to your environment. The model is `eleven_v4` by default (`--tts-model eleven_v4_turbo`, `eleven_v3` or `eleven_multilingual_v2` otherwise; a track keeps the model it was voiced with, so the default moving re-voices nothing). Style reaches it only on a tag model, v3 or v4 (`stylable` and `audioTags` follow the model chosen, not just the engine): it reads a short bracketed **audio tag** ahead of the sentence as performance direction, never the Gemini-shaped prose instruction, and never sent to any other ElevenLabs model, where the brackets would just be read aloud as words instead of shaping them. In the notes themselves, `[click]` is decklight's marker on every engine, never sent; `[pause]` and `[long pause]` are sent to a tag model as those tags, where the voice holds them itself, and held by decklight for every other voice (DECK_ANATOMY); any other bracketed tag reaches a tag model as written and is taken out for every other voice. An optional `--tts-stability creative|natural|robust` (v3 only; refused up front on any other model rather than silently doing nothing) trades how hard v3 follows the tag against how much it drifts, and is left alone entirely (no `voice_settings` sent at all) when unasked. Both the tag and the sentence it directs are billed as the characters actually sent, and it is metered in characters against a plan rate decklight cannot see, so the bridge reports characters rather than inventing a dollar figure. Audio is `pcm_24000` wrapped as WAV like every other engine; `--tts-format mp3` is the escape hatch for plans without PCM output and costs you the panel's `Record this deck…` offline recording and the lip-sync bridge, both of which read WAV, so it is opt-in, never a silent downgrade. `GET /ping` reports `{engine, model, voices, stylable}` and the picker offers exactly those voices, skipping the tone step when the engine cannot be told how to speak: a roster the bridge cannot pronounce would be a silent lie. **Which engine is itself a choice the deck can make**: `GET /tts/engines` lists every engine with its price note and, for the ones this machine cannot use, what is missing. **Voices by language** (ElevenLabs): the bridge reports the languages each account voice speaks, what ElevenLabs verified it for and what its labels name (`voiceLangs`), as the roster's fourth element, `[name, flavor, group, langs]`, shown after the flavor (`cloned · en fr`) and matched by the filter's `lang:` term. The voices view carries a **🌐 Language** row (the same `lang:` term, chosen from a list: the deck's language first, then what your voices speak with a count each, then the languages ElevenLabs speaks), and **opens on the deck's own language** (`<html lang>`, once per open, never English) because a deck translated to Spanish wants a Spanish voice. The account's voices are mostly English, so the view ends with **🔎 Find <language> voices in the ElevenLabs library…**: `GET /tts/voices/library?language=&search=` (the bridge asks `/v1/shared-voices`; free, changes nothing), each voice with ElevenLabs' own sample on ▶ (played as hosted, no synthesis). The first ⏎ on one ARMS it ("⏎ again adds “Mateo” to your ElevenLabs voices — it uses one of your plan's voice slots"); the second is `POST /tts/voices/library/add { engine, owner, id, name }` (`/v1/voices/add/{owner}/{id}`), the one call that changes the account; the roster is fetched afresh and the voice picked like any other. A plan with no free slot says so; any other engine answers 404 "has no voice library — ElevenLabs does". **The choice belongs to the tab, not the bridge**: one bridge serves every open tab (a deck, its speaker view, a second deck) so the bridge holds every engine it is asked for side by side, built once, and each tab names its engine on every request (`GET /tts/ping?engine=`, `/tts/voices?engine=`, `/tts/engines?engine=` marks that tab's engine current, `POST /tts { …, engine }`). A deck restores the engine its saved live voice was picked on by asking for it by name, and picking another in the picker does the same; neither switches anything for another tab. (They used to switch the bridge itself, and a second tab restoring its own engine on load silently took the voice from the first.) An engine this machine cannot run is refused with its blocker (409), and the deck speaks with the bridge's default and says so. A request naming no engine gets the default, the one the bridge started with; `POST /engine {engine}` still moves that default, for decks built before `?engine=`. The picker hangs off the voices view (`N` → live voice → *engine*), because the engine decides the whole roster and that is the screen where you notice the names are not the ones you wanted. It is drawn as a **bar at the top of the voices card**, above the filter: `ENGINE  elevenlabs  eleven_v4 … change engine ›`, the model named only where it is a real choice (ElevenLabs, Gemini, Chirp; for say, SAPI and piper it is merely the default voice), so the one control that decides every voice below it is not one more line among them; it is still the first row, so ↑/↓ and ⏎ reach it. The engines view lists the **ready** engines first, then the ones that **need setup**, each group under its own heading with its one-line fix, and the V panel's *Live voice* row names the engine and voice in use. **The readiness check is shared with write mode** (`engineStatus`, tools/tts-engines.mjs) and only its phrasing differs (a picker offering an engine `open` would have refused to start is a picker that lies) and it is asked BEFORE the engine is built, because gemini and chirp construct perfectly well against an unchecked project and fail on the first sentence, which mid-talk is a keypress into silence. A swap re-runs the same stale-voice rule a first probe does, and the response cache is keyed by engine name, so switching back replays instantly rather than re-billing. **Synthesis is cached on disk, for every engine including the paid ones** (`$XDG_CACHE_HOME/decklight/tts`, oldest dropped past 400 MB, `--no-cache` to opt out). The key is `(engine, model, format, voice, style, text)` and it is one function shared by the bridge and `decklight voiceover` (`clipKey`, tools/tts-cache.mjs), because those are separate processes and a deck previewed sentence by sentence in the browser and then batch-recorded was billed twice for identical words. The **model** is in the key, which is what makes caching a cloud engine safe rather than reckless: a server that changes its model underneath us produces a MISS, not a stale clip filed under today's name. **Style is in the key only when the engine can act on it**: every engine but gemini and ElevenLabs v3 ignores a delivery instruction, the deck usually sends none and the CLI defaults to a paragraph, so keying on a style nobody read would make identical audio hash two ways and miss every clip. `voiceover`'s manifest hash is that same key, shortened, so the folder-level skip and the content-level cache cannot disagree about whether a slide changed. **An engine that cannot be used is shown, dimmed, with the terminal command that fixes it** (hiding it would answer "where did ElevenLabs go?" with silence) and every fix is something you do in a terminal: the deck lists what is missing and never collects it, because a key or a project id typed into a web page (even one on loopback) is a different thing from one exported in a shell, and a deck under `--remote` is reachable from a phone. **The deck remembers the engine with its live voice**, and nothing else does: a voice name only means something on the engine it came from, so the deck's saved live voice carries its engine, and the next session's probe asks the bridge for that engine before it reads the roster: a bridge restarted on its default engine would otherwise find the saved voice missing and replace it. A saved engine the bridge refuses (the key is gone, the model deleted) is said out loud and the deck speaks with what the bridge has. `tts.json` is still never written from the deck: it is what the CLI and the wizard write and what the next write-mode start uses for *every* deck, and an experiment two minutes before a talk must not quietly become every deck's default. The bridge is probed when live narration **starts**, not only when the picker opens, and a saved voice the live bridge does not have is treated as **stale rather than chosen**: it came from a different engine (the star roster is the default, and an ElevenLabs key knows none of those names), so the bridge's first voice takes over, is persisted, and the swap is announced: a voice changing on its own must never be silent. **The voice already on the machine**: with no engine chosen, none saved, and no cloud project, write mode asks the operating system what it can speak with before defaulting to an engine that would only be skipped. **macOS** is read through `say -v '?'`: the roster mixes twenty years of engines, so voices are ranked and shown by **quality category**: *best* (a **Personal Voice** the user recorded, and **Siri** voices, recognisable on current macOS only by their sample sentence, the name markers having disappeared from the listing), *good* (Premium/Neural, then Enhanced), *average* (plain built-ins, and the **robots**: Albert, Zarvox, Bad News and the rest of the novelty/formant roster, pinned there by name and listed last, because they sort alphabetically first and once narrated decks by default). The categories render as labelled shelves in the picker, and only the deck's language gets them: the **novelty tail** (robots and the 2022 persona family: Eddy, Flo, Grandma, Grandpa, Reed, Rocko, Sandy, Shelley) and **every other-language voice** each fold to a single expandable row (`‣ novelty voices (26)` · `‣ other languages (140)`), expanded per picker-open and never persisted: a real Mac answers ~184 names, and ~165 of them were the reason "which voice is good here" was unanswerable. When no best-tier voice is installed, the BEST shelf holds an actionable row: *⬇ Get Siri voices — free download*, whose commit asks the bridge's `POST /voices/install` to open the macOS voice-download pane: darwin-only, loopback-only, and the argv is a **frozen constant** (`open x-apple.systempreferences:…`) that no byte of any request reaches; the download itself stays a human clicking through, which is the point of the pane. This is the ONE exec a deck may trigger, and it opens a Settings pane and nothing else. A Mac whose best voice speaks Italian must still not narrate an English deck with it. A Siri voice hiding behind a **duplicate display name** (how current macOS lists them) is dropped rather than offered: `say -v` addresses by name, so that row would play a different voice than it names. **macOS 27** changed the listing twice over, and the roster reads it as it is: a plain voice now carries its language (`Daniel (English (UK))`, `Samantha (English (US))`), so a plain twin of `Daniel (Enhanced)` is recognised by its **base name**, under both a quality marker and a language pair, rather than its whole name, which let every one of them through; and some voices are printed twice, line for line, and are offered once (a Siri voice under a plain voice's name differs by its sample, so it is not a repeat and stays the rule above's to drop). Names keep their suffix in the picker, because the suffix selects a different build: `say -v Daniel` speaks the Enhanced one. The bridge still accepts a voice by its **bare name** (`Samantha`), which `say -v` answers to, so a deck that saved one, or a track recorded as one, keeps working on a Mac whose listing grew the suffix; a name with no voice under it is still refused rather than quietly swapped for the system default. A machine with **no best-tier voice** is told, on the caveat channel every engine already has (the bridge's startup line, the deck's toast): Siri voices are a free download away, with the Settings path and the `open "x-apple.systempreferences:…"` command that gets there, suggested, never run, because only a person can click through a voice download. **Windows** is read through PowerShell: the **WinRT** stack first (`Windows.Media.SpeechSynthesis`, projected from Windows PowerShell 5.1), because that is where the **natural** voices live (Aria, Jenny, Guy, the neural ones Narrator and Edge use, and the closest thing Windows has to Siri); `System.Speech`, the 2006 API that sees only David, Zira and Mark, is the fallback for a PowerShell that cannot project. Synthesis goes through whichever stack listed the voice, and a name the machine does not have throws rather than falling back to the default voice. A machine with no natural voice installed is told where they are (Settings → Accessibility → Narrator → *Add natural voices*) on the same caveat channel and with the same picker row as macOS's Siri hint (`start ms-settings:easeofaccess-narrator`, a frozen argv, is the one exec that row may trigger there). Both become real bridge engines (`--tts-engine say|sapi`), synthesizing 24 kHz PCM in a WAV like every other engine, `stylable: false` so the picker skips the tone step. **Android** is detected and refused with the reason: Termux reports `android` and runs Node, but Android's TextToSpeech is an in-process Java API with no command line behind it, so there is nothing for a bridge to call: the honest path there is a recorded track, which needs no bridge. A native voice is the ONLY engine claimed automatically, because it is the only one that needs no setup at all; **piper is never auto-selected even when it is on PATH**, since it needs a toolchain and a ~120 MB model and that choice belongs to the first-run wizard, which saves it. Any `--tts-engine`, `--project`, `GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT` or saved config wins outright and detection is never consulted. When a machine has nothing, the skip line says what THIS machine lacks and names piper. **A missing piper voice is caught before the bridge starts, not by it**: piper installed and chosen but with no model on disk used to bring the bridge up looking healthy and fail on the first sentence: a keypress into silence, for a file that was never there. write mode now checks, names the model, its directory and its size, and on a **TTY** offers to fetch it; accepting re-plans so the bridge joins the session, declining or a non-TTY run keeps the skip line. **Nothing is ever downloaded silently.** The command it runs is chosen for the machine (`tools/tts-engines.mjs`): a python that can already import piper gets `python -m piper.download_voices`, and otherwise `uvx --from piper-tts` runs it inside the isolated venv `uv tool install piper-tts` creates: the plain form cannot work there, which is why it used to fail with `ModuleNotFoundError` on a machine that had just followed decklight's own install instruction. **First-run setup**: the first time the bridge cannot start (a bare `decklight tts`, or write mode about to skip the voice because nothing is configured), decklight asks a few plain questions on the terminal (TTY only) instead of exiting with the engine's error: the engine, with the free/offline/cloud trade-offs stated and the default following what the machine already has (an `$ELEVENLABS_API_KEY` outranks everything: nobody exports one by accident; else piper on `PATH`, else a project visible in the environment or gcloud config), then only that engine's prerequisites: a GCP project id (prefilled, validated with the same rule the flags enforce) plus a credentials probe for the cloud engines; for piper the install and voice-model download commands, run only on an explicit yes; for elevenlabs nothing at all when the key is exported (the roster answers "which voice", so it is not asked) and otherwise where to make a key and why setup will not save it for you. Setup proves the choice with one real synthesis of the picker's preview sentence, reporting duration and the engine's cost note, and only a config that has spoken is saved, to `~/.config/decklight/tts.json` (`$XDG_CONFIG_HOME` honored), which both `decklight tts` and `decklight <deck>` read from then on; precedence is flags > environment > saved config > built-in default. `decklight tts --setup` re-runs the questions, deleting the file resets to first-run, and non-TTY invocations never prompt; exit codes and messages stay as they were. **The voice is the clock**: auto-advance is driven by narration finishing, so when the voice CANNOT be played (a quota 429, an unreachable bridge, a browser that blocked autoplay, a recorded track with no file for this slide), the deck **stops advancing** rather than walking the talk past slides nobody has heard, and says why in a message (`I`) naming the cause and the way out (`--tts-engine chirp` for a 429, `decklight tts` for a dead bridge, one click for blocked audio). Narration switches itself off; `V` retries: the picker drills tracks → voice (30 prebuilt Gemini/Chirp names, one piper model, an OS voice, or the ElevenLabs account's own roster) → tone, on any engine `/tts/ping` reports as `stylable` (six presets or a custom typed instruction: a prompt prefix for gemini, a short bracketed audio tag for ElevenLabs v3 and v4). Every voice row carries a ▶ **preview** that speaks an editable test sentence (default "Hey, this is Decklight", input at the top of the voices view, ↺ restores the default) through the bridge in that voice; tone rows preview the drafted voice in that delivery style, and the custom-tone input has a ▶ to audition a typed instruction before committing it. After a preview plays, the rest of its roster prefetches sequentially in the background (all voices neutrally, or all tones for the drafted voice). Two preview caches: the default sentence's cache is permanent once built; the custom sentence's cache holds exactly one sentence and is swapped (old audio freed) when the text changes. **Build-synced auto-advance**: the ⟨CLICK⟩ markers that segment the notes for the speaker view segment the audio too: segment k is synthesized as its own clip and narrates build step k (0 = arrival); when a clip ends the deck reveals the next build, and after the last segment it moves to the next slide, so a deck with well-segmented notes presents itself beat by beat. **More segments than builds** (the commonest case being a slide with no `data-build` at all, which has exactly one step) used to drop every surplus segment: never synthesized, never spoken, never mentioned, while the speaker view went on displaying it, so the presenter read a cue the deck would never say. The surplus now rides on the **last** step. There is nothing left to reveal, so the marker becomes a beat rather than a build trigger: the same call `tools/video.mjs` makes for a count that does not line up, and the same one the panel's `Record this deck…` stitching and the viseme sidecars make, each giving a folded segment a segment-sized silence rather than a sentence-sized one so a recorded take breathes where the live one does. The counts disagreeing is still nearly always an authoring slip, so it is named once per slide in the message log (`I`) and the debug log, never toasted, because it is a note about the deck rather than something to act on mid-sentence. The opposite mismatch needs no rule: fewer segments than builds already advances after a short beat, losing nothing. A segment with no words advances after a short beat; a slide with no notes is skipped; manual navigation mid-clip wins over the pending advance and re-syncs the voice to the new step. **Between sentences the voice breathes 0.25s by default** (the pause a reader takes at a full stop, which a chain of separately synthesized clips otherwise lacks), overridable for the deck (`narration: { sentencePause: 0.5 }`) and per slide (`data-narration-sentence-pause="0"`, where an explicit zero is zero); a typo falls through to the next tier, never to silence. the panel's `Record this deck…` stitches the same breath between sentences, so a recorded take paces a slide exactly as the live voice did. **Every pause resolves the same three tiers**: the slide's attribute, else the deck's `narration: { sentencePause, beatPause, slidePause }`, else the built-in default (0.25s between sentences, **0.5s between builds, 1s before the slide turns**), opt-*out* rather than opt-in, because a deck that talks straight through its own punctuation is the behaviour nobody asks for on purpose; so a deck can breathe uniformly from one line of config and a single slide can still say otherwise, down to `"0"`; an unparseable value at either tier falls through, never to silence. **`data-narration-beat-pause="0.5"`** is the same breath **between builds**: each bullet lands, holds that many seconds, and only then does the voice reveal the next: honored at play time for live and recorded tracks alike, never baked into a recording, and malformed values read as no pause, exactly like its end-of-slide sibling. **`⟨PAUSE⟩` anywhere in the notes** (#560) is a let-it-sink-in hold at that spot: **two beat pauses** (1s by default), so it follows the slide's `data-narration-beat-pause` and the deck's `narration.beatPause`, and a slide that zeroes the one zeroes the other. It is never spoken, captioned, subtitled or written to PowerPoint notes. It belongs to the words before it (between sentences, attached to one, or on a line of its own) except that one before any words of a ⟨CLICK⟩ beat holds after that build lands, so `A. ⟨PAUSE⟩ ⟨CLICK⟩ B.` holds then reveals and `A. ⟨CLICK⟩ ⟨PAUSE⟩ B.` reveals then holds. Mid-sentence, it cuts the sentence there, and several in a row hold that many times. The live voice holds it between clips. Because it sits *inside* a beat, where recorded playback has no seam to hold at, every recording **bakes it in** as silence: the deck's recorder (WAV and viseme sidecar alike), and the synthesis core, which voices the words on either side as separate clips and joins them (a clip in anything but WAV needs ffmpeg to decode). A narrated video therefore gets it from the audio's measured length, and a silent frame adds it to its hold itself. The marker's position is part of the text a slide's hash is taken over, so adding or moving one makes a recorded slide stale and the export re-voices it. A slide with no marker hashes exactly as it did before markers existed, and a change of pause *length* does not stale a take, just as a changed `sentencePause` does not. The `.txt` script beside a clip keeps its markers, since they are part of what was recorded; lip-sync hands Rhubarb the words alone, and subtitles leave each hold uncaptioned. A beat of nothing but `⟨PAUSE⟩` has no take of its own: live it holds instead of the usual short silent beat, and the recorders give it no file. **`data-narration="hold"`** on a section marks it interactive (quiz, exercise, live demo): narration plays whatever notes it has and builds still sync, but the deck never auto-advances *off* it: the presenter moves on manually and narration resumes on the next slide. The spoken unit is a **sentence**: each ⟨CLICK⟩ segment splits into sentences, every sentence is its own TTS call and cache entry (slide, segment, sentence, voice, style), playback chains them and advances only after the segment's last one, so first audio arrives after one short synthesis, never a whole paragraph. While narration is on, a **lookahead buffer** keeps the next 10 sentences synthesized in the background (a few parallel low-priority workers, window re-derived from the current position, results landing in the same cache); an unreachable bridge toasts once and pauses the buffer until the next event. the panel's `Record this deck…` records per-slide `slide-NN.wav` files **stitched from this same sentence cache** (short silences joining sentences and builds): already-heard clips cost nothing, only unheard sentences synthesize. **They are written next to the deck, not downloaded**: in write mode each stitched slide is POSTed to the server that already owns the deck file (`/deck/edit/narration/record`), which writes it, and the character's `slide-NN.visemes.json` sidecar, into the deck's own `narration.files` folder, or `voiceover/` when the deck names none. That is the only place `narration.files` can point at and the only place `decklight bundle` looks, so the recording is finished where the next command reads it; the browser's download folder is neither, and is now the FALLBACK for the cases nothing may write to disk: a `file://` deck, or one served read-only by `decklight <deck> --no-trust`. The player names the folder and never the file: `/deck/edit/narration/record` builds `slide-NN.wav` itself and refuses a folder that resolves outside the served root, so the write surface a recording opens is one directory of one deck. The card that ends the recording says which of the two happened and, on disk, the exact `narration: { files, ext: 'wav' }` line that plays the set back. **`P` pauses/resumes** narration: audio freezes mid-sentence and resumes exactly there, and auto-advance holds while paused. Captions (`C`) follow the voice **sentence by sentence** during live narration (segment-level when narration is off). With nothing configured, `V` opens the picker instead of failing. **The deck announces its own voice**: when a recorded track is configured, a small pill fades in bottom-center about a second after load: *🔊 this deck has a voice-over — `V` plays it*, and clicking it starts narration (the click is itself the user gesture, so the audio is never the blocked-autoplay case). It fades out on its own after ~12s and is written to the message log (`I`) rather than toasted, since the pill is already on screen saying it. It never appears in `?print`, in embedded previews (the theme picker and slide finder boot real decks in iframes), in a render (`?capture`, below), on a load the URL sent past the first build of the first slide (a link into the middle of a talk is somebody presenting, not a first view, the rule the welcome card keeps), under `?voiceover` (which starts on the first gesture anyway), while captions (`C`) hold that same corner, or once narration has been started on this deck, remembered per deck path like every other narration preference. Without it the only proactive surface was the touch sound button, which CSS shows on `pointer: coarse` alone, so a viewer on a laptop could only learn the deck talks from a key nobody had mentioned. Files are generated by **`decklight voiceover <deck.html>`** (the dispatcher spawns `tools/voiceover.mjs`, which arg-parses at load and so cannot be imported, the same way `video --voiceover` runs it), the headless counterpart of the panel's `Record this deck…`: the notes, voiced as written, by `--engine piper` (local neural TTS; `--voice` = piper model name), `chirp`, `gemini` or `elevenlabs`. **One synthesis core produces every machine-voiced track** (`tools/narration-synth.mjs`, #555): `voiceover` is its command line, and the export that voices a range before rendering goes through it too, so a track made by one can be refreshed by another. It owns sentence synthesis through the shared clip cache, the ⟨CLICK⟩ beat files, stitching a slide from its beats, encoding, and the skip-by-hash that makes a rerun free; `tools/narration-manifest.mjs` (what a slide's text and hash are) and `tools/tts-cache.mjs` (has anyone paid for these words) stay the identity layer beside it. **A track keeps its format**: `wav`, `m4a` or `mp3`, read off its files: a track the deck's recorder made is wav, and refreshing one of its slides writes wav, where `voiceover` used to write m4a beside it, re-voiced in piper; a fresh track is m4a unless `--format` says otherwise, and wav is written directly, with no encoder. **A folder that already holds a track is refreshed AS that track**: its engine, voice, style and (for ElevenLabs and Gemini) model are the defaults, a flag overrides each, and a header field the core does not own (the recorder's `recorder: 'deck'`) is carried. A slide whose hash still matches and whose file (in the track's format) is on disk is kept, whoever made it. A **`--slides` run in another voice is refused** (a header names one voice, and the slides outside the range would read as stale against it), with the way out: voice the whole track, or give the other voice its own folder. The microphone recorder is untouched: a human voice is the browser's. The built-in speech-synthesis voices were removed: not good enough. `?voiceover` starts narration on the first user gesture. `instance.toggleNarration()` programmatically.
- **Enhance the script** (`decklight enhance deck.html --slides <n|a-b|a,b> | --all [--dry-run] [--agent <name>]`; in write mode also `V` → *Enhance the script — ElevenLabs audio tags…* → this slide / every slide with notes, and the two `/` palette rows *Enhance this slide's / every slide's voiceover script…*; and **✨ add audio tags** in the notes editor, which enhances the text IN THE BOX, unsaved edits included, through `POST /deck/edit/enhance/text { text }` and puts the checked answer back in the box to read, edit and save with ⌘⏎, writing nothing itself; its tooltip names the agent and says the text goes to it): adds the audio tags ElevenLabs v4 performs (`[thoughtful]`, `[sighs]`, `[excited]`, DECK_ANATOMY) to the speaker notes. The prompt is **ElevenLabs' own**, the one its [best practices](https://elevenlabs.io/docs/overview/capabilities/text-to-speech/best-practices#prompting-eleven-v4) publish for having an LLM enhance a script, verbatim (`cli/enhance.mjs`), plus a short decklight addendum: one narrator, keep every `[click]` on its own line and every `[pause]`, keep the line breaks, answer with the script only. The **locally installed agent** answers it with the same read-only argv the commit subjects use (one ask per slide, three at a time, two minutes each) and is never let near the file. **Decklight writes, and only what passes a check**: after tags, markers, case and punctuation are set aside the words must be the same words in the same order (the prompt allows only tags, capitals, `!`, `?` and `…`), the `[click]` beats the same count, and no beat may lose a `[pause]` (adding `[short pause]` / `[long pause]` is allowed). A slide whose answer fails is left exactly as it was and the result says why; the paragraphs stay a blank line apart. In write mode the run is `POST /deck/edit/enhance { slides: [n…] | 'all' }` (409 while another enhance or an agent edit runs; 400 naming what is missing with no agent; 422 with no notes to enhance), progress rides SSE `enhance` events into one rewriting row, and the answers land in **one** edit against the deck as it is when the run ends: a slide edited meanwhile keeps the edit, so `Z` takes the whole run back; the closing line (*enhanced 3 slides — Z undoes · slide 5 left as it was: …*) is said again after the reload the write causes. The notes are sent to that agent, and most agents are cloud services; the CLI's help says so. **Written for the ear** (`--spoken`; V → *🗣 Write the script for the ear…*; palette *Write this slide's / this module's / every slide's notes for the ear*; the notes editor's *🗣 write it for the ear*): the other rewrite, with its own prompt (`SPOKEN_PROMPT`, cli/enhance.mjs): terse, written-style notes turned into sentences a person would say out loud ("Fluffed a line? Backspace retakes it." → "And if you fluff a line, just press Backspace to take it again."), every fact kept, nothing added, symbols said as words, about as long as it was. It REWORDS, so its check is not "same words": the same `[click]` beats (the builds), no `[pause]` lost, no beat emptied, and a length between half and two and a half times the original: an answer outside that is commentary or a dropped point, and the slide is left as it was. **Every rewrite offers three scopes**: this slide, this slide's **module** (the `data-module` chapter it is in, DECK_ANATOMY, offered only when there is one), or every slide with notes. In the notes editor ✨ and 🗣 each ask which first: *this slide* lands in the box to read and ⌘⏎-save (nothing is written by pressing it); a module or the deck is rewritten in the file in one edit, which Z takes back: refused, with a sentence, while the box holds unsaved edits. **⌘⏎ saves the notes editor from wherever the focus is** while it is up: the box, a button in it, its background, or (docked) the slide beside it. A save that silently did nothing, with the text still in the box, read as a save (#646). A typing box of its own (the agent ask, the commit window, the content editor) keeps its own ⌘⏎, and the read-only card (`--no-trust`, `--no-trust`) has no save to reach. **A save does not reload the page**: the notes came from the page, so `POST /deck/edit/slide/notes` writes quietly (the watcher skips the reload for exactly those bytes) and broadcasts the slide's new notes as a `notes` event. Every open view of the deck puts them in place: narration and captions read the new text, the speaker view re-reads it, and a notes card open on that slide in another tab follows them unless it holds edits of its own. The page that saved keeps its card exactly as it was, caret and scroll included, and its own echo (`from` names the page) leaves the box as typed rather than as the file normalised it. Any other change to the file (an undo, an agent's edit, an editor's save) still reloads. Should a save's write reload anyway, the card is reopened on that slide with the caret and the scroll restored, docked or floating as it was, from a record kept in sessionStorage for a minute; Esc or × leaves none, so a card closed on purpose stays closed. **● unsaved** sits beside the heading, at full strength while the heading is dimmed, whenever the box differs from the file; it reads **saving…** while the request is in flight and clears once the box matches the file (a save that wrote, or one that had nothing to write). A failed save leaves it up. **Reset, and before / after, in the notes editor**: two buttons against the notes AS LAST SAVED (the deck reloads after every save, so its own notes are the file's). **↺ reset** puts the box back to them: every change dropped, typed or drafted by the agent. **⇄ before / after** swaps the box for a word-level diff (src/core/worddiff.js: a longest common subsequence over words and the whitespace between them, so line breaks and `[click]` lines survive; whole lines past a size cap), removed words struck through, added words highlighted, with the counts; ⌘⏎ still saves the after, Esc or the button goes back to editing. Both are inert while the box says what the file says. When a rewrite lands in the box the before/after button is lit until looked at: the way to read what an agent changed before it is written.
- **Character** (`V` → Character…, or palette → Character…): an animated narrator in a stage corner whose lips sync to the narration, fully offline, no cloud service. Two render modes behind one overlay (`.decklight-character`, persisted per deck): **🎭 2D character**: a layered SVG bust (theme-aware clothing/backdrop, blink, idle sway) with one mouth group per Rhubarb shape (`A–H` + `X` rest); a 30 Hz loop maps `narrAudio.currentTime` onto a **viseme timeline** and shows the matching `[data-mouth]` group, so pause (`P`) and voice speed (`<`/`>`) sync for free. **🎥 Neural video**: a muted picture-in-picture `<video>` talking head (audio always comes from the narration element; muted video is immune to autoplay policy) generated by Wav2Lip or SadTalker on the presenter's own GPU, kept on what is **heard**, not on the audio element's clock: its target is `audio.currentTime` minus the output delay the browser reports (`AudioContext.outputLatency` + `baseLatency`, tens of ms through speakers, far more through Bluetooth) minus `narration.character.lipOffsetMs` (a hand adjustment, positive = lips later); within 250 ms the video's rate is nudged ±8% until it is within 15 ms, and only a bigger gap seeks (`lipCorrection`), so the mouth never stutters on a seek per tick. Measured on a real clip: the old ±150 ms seek-only rule held the lips a steady ~35 ms ahead of the element clock (worse by the output delay) and let them drift to 212 ms; now a median of 13 ms. The debug log (`D`) names the output delay it used. It mirrors the voice's play/pause/rate. Timelines and clips come from the **lipsync bridge** (`decklight lipsync`; the deck asks its own origin for `/lipsync/*`, which `open` proxies to the bridge it started, and falls back to `http://127.0.0.1:8789` when opened from disk; override via `narration.character.bridgeUrl`; CORS-open, 127.0.0.1-only, disk-cached in `~/.cache/decklight/lipsync` keyed by audio+params hash): `GET /ping` reports which engines actually resolve (the picker greys out the rest), `POST /viseme?text=` (raw WAV body → timeline JSON v1: `{v, duration, cues: [{t, v}]}`, start-time cues, each shape holds until the next), `POST /video?engine=&portrait=` (raw WAV → muted faststart MP4; strictly serial GPU queue), `GET /portrait?name=` (the still the clips are made from). **Set up once, remembered**: `decklight lipsync --wav2lip-dir <checkout> --wav2lip-ckpt <wav2lip_gan.pth> --python <its venv python> --portrait me=<photo> --save` writes `~/.config/decklight/lipsync.json` (`$XDG_CONFIG_HOME` honored) and exits; the bridge reads it under any flag it is not given, `open` starts the bridge for a saved talking head with no flags (rhubarb or not), and `doctor` reports it: one check (`videoSetup`, tools/lipsync-config.mjs) says what stands in the way for all three and the deck's character row, in the same words. **How a clip is made from a photo** (Wav2Lip): a portrait larger than 1024 px is scaled down (S3FD misses the face in a full-size phone photo); its face is found ONCE with Wav2Lip's own detector and cached, then passed as `--box` so no clip detects again; the still is cropped square around the head so the face fills the round overlay (and renders faster); and because Wav2Lip redraws the whole face box at 96 px, only a feathered ellipse over the mouth and chin is taken from its output: everything else stays the sharp photograph (limited-range `yuv420p`). **A portrait can be filmed** (`--portrait me=clip.mp4`, `.mov` `.m4v` `.webm` `.mkv`, 5–10 s facing the camera, mouth relaxed, not talking; its sound is ignored): made ONCE per film and cached by content (`prepareClip`): its first 10 s at 25 fps, scaled like a photo, its face found in one frame a second, cropped square around the head, and played **forward then back** so the loop Wav2Lip makes of it for a long sentence never snaps the head back to the start (Wav2Lip loops its `--face` frames from the first, so a plain film jumps). If the head held still (a face in every sampled frame, its centre wandering less than a tenth of the face width (`steadyBox`)), the union of those boxes goes to Wav2Lip as `--box` and only the mouth is laid over the film, frame for frame (Wav2Lip's frame i is the loop's frame i); a head that moves is left to Wav2Lip's own per-frame detection, the whole face redrawn and softer, and the bridge says so. The film's first frame is the still under the video. Veo is not asked to animate a film, and SadTalker gets that first frame. The bridge prepares every portrait when it starts, not on the talk's first sentence. **Or filmed in the deck**: V → Character → *📹 Film yourself* opens the camera in the medallion's own circle (mirrored, as a face expects; the film is not), with what to do (face the camera, mouth relaxed, don't talk, head still), then a 3-2-1 and `narration.character.filmSeconds` (default 8) of recording, a review of the take (⏎ use it, R retake, Esc cancel), and `POST /portrait?name=filmed` (the MediaRecorder's own mp4 or webm) to the lip-sync bridge. The bridge prepares it like any film, keeps it beside `lipsync.json` (`portraits/filmed.mp4`, a retake replacing it), remembers it there, with the bridge's own Wav2Lip setup where none was saved, and offers it at once, no restart; the deck switches the character to it. A film with no face is refused in a sentence and the card offers a retake; a camera refused or absent is said as what to do. The row shows whenever the bridge can take a film (`/ping` `film`, or `filmWhy`), and a bridge with Wav2Lip and no portrait yet still starts, so filming is how the first portrait gets made (the video row then points at it). **A recorded track needs no pre-rendered clips**: in video mode the deck plays `slide-NN.mp4` beside the track when it exists (`decklight lipsync` renders them as a batch); when the server says it does not, a track you just recorded, and the bridge makes video, the head gets a clip made from the very audio file that plays (`POST /video` with that file; one per beat on a beat-paced track, keyed by the file, cached by content on the bridge), synced to it exactly as a live sentence is, with the rest of the slide and the next one warmed ahead in playing order. Until a clip lands the photo shows, never the previous beat's mouth. A request that cannot be made at all (a deck on file://) trusts the sidecar, as before; the check is a GET with the body dropped, since `open` answers HEAD 405. **The medallion is never black**: in video mode the character shows that same still (`/portrait`) under the video, and the video fades in only while a clip is playing (`playing` → shown, `ended`/`emptied` → the photo again): a clip that is still rendering when its sentence is spoken leaves the photo, not an empty circle. In **live voice** mode the sentence is the unit here too: the lookahead buffer that keeps 10 sentences of audio warm hands each synthesized sentence's WAV to the bridge, so lip-sync data prefetches through the same window, workers, and promise-cache dedup as the voice. And playback NEVER waits on lip-sync (a late timeline lands mid-sentence; until then the **fallback** animates: `amplitude` (default) drives a coarse 4-shape mouth from a WebAudio analyser, `hide` shows the idle face). In **recorded** mode the overlay loads per-slide sidecars from the track's dir: `slide-NN.visemes.json` / `slide-NN.mp4`, generated by `tools/lipsync.mjs` (rhubarb + optional SadTalker/Wav2Lip batch, hash-incremental like voiceover.mjs), preferring an inline `<script type="application/json" data-decklight-visemes="slide-NN">` block when present (`decklight bundle` inlines viseme JSONs; fetch is blocked on `file://`). the panel's `Record this deck…` exports `slide-NN.visemes.json` alongside each stitched WAV (same sentence cache, same silence gaps) so a recorded set is character-ready with zero extra synthesis. **Solo** (`N` → Character… → Solo, or palette → "Character solo"): the narrator takes the stage: the slide content, progress bar and slide number step aside (`visibility: hidden` on `.decklight-stage`, which the overlay is a sibling of) and the character centers, sized to the stage (`--character-solo-size`, default `min(74vh, 74vw)`). A talking-head slide with nothing else on it, without authoring one. Works with either render mode, persists per deck alongside `mode`, and is a *presentation* state, not a layout: the root's `decklight-solo` class is only ever set while the character is actually on screen, so turning the character off, or stopping the narration, can never leave a blank stage behind. Turning solo ON shows the character immediately (idling, blinking) rather than waiting for `V`. **Motion** (`decklight lipsync --veo`, SPEC PRESENTING): Wav2Lip repaints the lip region and leaves the rest of its source untouched: from a still photo that is a frozen stare with a moving mouth. With `--veo` the bridge animates each portrait ONCE through Veo image-to-video on Vertex AI (`veo-3.1-lite-generate-001` by default; 4/6/8s; `generateAudio: false`, the deck owns the voice, and it is the cheaper rate) and gives Wav2Lip that clip as its `--face`, so the head turns and blinks under the new mouth. The unit is the PORTRAIT, not the sentence: the player asks for video once per sentence through a 10-sentence lookahead, so a Veo call per sentence would be both unusably slow (~40s) and a runaway bill. Instead each portrait's motion clip is bought on first use, deduped in flight, and cached on disk (keyed by portrait+model+seconds+prompt), so a deck of any length costs exactly one call per portrait, ever. Delete the cache entry to re-roll the performance. The clip is cropped square around the head (the overlay is a circle) and scaled to 640px (s3fd misses faces in a large frame). SadTalker is excluded: it generates its own head motion and wants a still. A refusal by Veo's person-generation filter surfaces as an error naming the cause, never an empty clip; a misconfigured `--veo` disables only itself and leaves visemes and still-portrait video working. It is the only part of the bridge that leaves the machine, so it is opt-in, and write mode passes it straight through. Config (`narration.character`, all optional): `mode: 'off'|'viseme'|'video'` (picker overrides, persisted), `solo: boolean` (default false; picker overrides, persisted), `bridgeUrl`, `position: 'br'|'bl'|'tr'|'tl'`, `size` (design px, default 220), `svg` (custom art: inline markup, `'#id'` ref, or URL, contract: `[data-mouth="A"…"X"]` groups; optional `[data-eyelid]`/`[data-idle]` get the blink/sway), `sprites: {A: url, …}` (image frames instead), `engine`/`portrait` (video mode; Wav2Lip suits live: static pose, seamless per-sentence cuts; SadTalker suits batch clips), `fallback: 'amplitude'|'hide'`. Bundles: viseme JSONs inline (1–4 KB/slide); per-slide MP4s stay external with a CLI notice: video cannot inline sanely.
- **Command palette**: `/` opens a Claude-style palette: every command with its shortcut, type-to-filter, `Enter` runs, `Esc` clears then closes. **With no query the list is the deck's doors, not its every command** (`src/core/palette.js`): rows that share a `group` fold into one row each (**Export & share…** holds PowerPoint, the PDFs, the video, the bundle and Publish; **Slide…** the five slide operations; **Print…** the three print views; **Write for the ear…** and **Add audio tags…** their three scopes), a group row opens its rows under a **← back** row (`⌫` on an empty query leaves it too, and the input says `Export & share ›`), and the write-mode rows sit together under one **write mode** header rather than each carrying a `(dev)` suffix. Typing searches every command flat, groups included, so `handout` finds the PDF handout in one step, and a row found that way carries an **author** tag when it needs the edit server. Argument commands (Theme…, Font…, Narration voice…, Module…, Find slide…) drill into their pickers; contextual commands appear only when applicable (Save generated theme, Browse marketplace themes, the Configure ⟨engine⟩ wizard rows, Module). Inline arguments work: `goto 27`, or just typing `27`, surfaces a “Go to slide 27 / N” row (clamped to the deck), and selecting the bare “Go to slide…” command keeps the palette open with `goto ` prefilled. Text matching no command falls back to a “Search slides for …” row that opens the finder with the query prefilled.
- **Settings** (`SETTINGS`): the palette's **Settings…** row (also found by typing `config`, `preferences` or `version`) opens a small panel of facts about what this deck is running, and the home for any later knob not worth a key of its own. A row with no action is a **fact**, shown at full strength with no pointer cursor, never greyed out the way an unavailable command is: dimming it would say "not available to you" about something that is merely information. Today it holds one, the **runtime version**, which is the first thing a bug report asks for and which nothing in the player could previously answer: the version rides the bundle's banner, a comment you would have to open the file to read. It shows the **build**, not just the version (`0.9.0+107.g6dad7a3`, the same stamp `decklight --version` prints, plain on a release build), because "which 0.9.0?" is the question a bug report is asking. It is baked in at build time (`__DECKLIGHT_VERSION__`), formatted by the CLI's own `versionLine` from the `git describe` the build already records, so the panel and the terminal can never spell one build two ways; a render assertion checks the panel against the runtime's own `Decklight.version`. **⏎ or a click copies it** ("copied 0.9.0+108.gcca64bd"), because a version retyped into a bug report arrives with a typo in it: the async clipboard first, the old select-and-copy where that is refused (a deck on `file://` is not a secure context everywhere), and if both are refused the toast carries the text itself. `instance.settings.open()` / `.close()` / `.rows()` is the programmatic door, and `rows()` answers `[{ label, value?, run? }]` without opening an overlay, so a headless probe can read the version without driving the UI.
- **Slide finder**: reached from the palette (`/` → Enter, or the search fallback): a find-a-slide overlay with the picker's anatomy, a query bar and result list on the left, a live preview of the selected slide on the right. Typing filters as an AND over the query's words against each slide's text; slides whose **title** contains every word rank first, body-only matches follow, and every match is listed as `<slide number> · <title>` (slides without a heading fall back to their leading text). `↑/↓`/hover browse (the preview swaps live), `Enter`/click jumps to the slide, `Backspace` edits, `Esc` clears the query then closes. The preview reuses the picker's lazy embedded-deck mechanism: the iframe boots once (carrying the active theme, generated/custom included via `?gen=`), then selections postMessage `{__decklightPreview: {goto: [slide, step]}}` into it; no reload per candidate. **A multi-module deck's finder is its outline**: on a deck carrying in-file
`data-module` markers (`DECK_ANATOMY`) and nothing typed, `G` shows each chapter
as a `▸ <title> — module · N slides` row folding its own slides, from the
marked slide up to the next marker, underneath it, with any slides before the
first chapter left top-level. It opens folded except for the chapter you are
standing in, cursor on your slide. `→` unfolds a chapter (and steps into an open
one), `←` folds it (and climbs from a slide back to its chapter), and the
`▸`/`▾` glyph folds in place on a click; `⏎` or a click on the row itself still
goes there: a `goto()` in this file, which the caption says (`module — <title>
(slide N of this deck)`). **Typing drops the fold** and shows the flat ranked
list, chapters matching as titles, because a query's answer is sorted by
relevance and a tree would hide half of it; clearing the query returns to the
outline unfolded on the chapter of whatever was selected. Only for the in-file
shape: a `config.playlist` deck is untouched, the two never coexisting. Before
this a 150-slide merge showed ten chapter rows bolted onto a flat dump of every
slide. The chapters were visible, but they organised nothing.
- **Playlist (multi-deck navigation)**: `Decklight.init({ playlist: { modules: [{title, href}…], index: n } })`. Advancing past the last build of the last slide navigates to the next module (`href#/1/0`); reversing before slide 1 goes to the previous module's end (`href#/999/999`, oversized hashes clamp to the last slide/step). The other modules appear as rows in the **slide finder** (`G`): marked `▸ <title> — module`, previewed like any slide, and `Enter` loads that file; the slide-number chrome shows the module title and opens the finder on click. There is no separate module menu: "go somewhere" is one question with one answer. Works on `file://` with relative hrefs; embedded instances never chain.
- **History** (`H`, write mode; `R` is the same door). Every commit that touched this deck, newest first, with a live preview of each and a `⇑` on the ones that exist nowhere but this machine. Each row carries what that version **was** and what it **changed**: a slide count, and `+added` / `−removed` lines in the two colours every diff has used for forty years: because a hash and a subject cannot tell "tightened the wording" apart from "cut four slides", and that is exactly the distinction you are scanning for. The preview has its own transport (`⇤ ← → ⇥`, and the same keys), so a version is judged by walking it rather than by its first slide; the deck behind the overlay does not move. Under the transport, a **caption** spells out the selected row in a sentence (hash, age, subject, slide and line counts), and it is **off by default**: it repeats a row you can already read, right under the slide you are trying to judge. `C` (the deck's own captions key, meaning the same thing one layer up) or the `CC` button at the end of the transport shows and hides it, and the choice is remembered per browser (`decklight-history-captions`), because it is how you like this overlay rather than a fact about any one deck. **The preview is what this overlay is for**, so the layout is built never to fight it: a wide panel, the preview on a 16:9 `aspect-ratio` because a deck is 1280×720 and a letterboxed sliver judges nothing, and a rail that is structurally incapable of squeezing it. That last part is the lesson from shipping it squeezed: a flex item's default `min-width: auto` forbids shrinking below its own min-content width, so a rail whose row put four things on one line, under a `nowrap` title bar, made `flex: 0 0 300px` a suggestion its content could veto, and the pane rendered 57px. The row is two lines now: subject, then a dimmer line of hash, badges and a compact age. The title bar wraps, and `min-width: 0` is on the column, so nothing in the rail wants to be wide and the preview's size stops being an accident of how somebody worded a commit message. A preview that cannot be produced **says so in the frame**: `/deck/edit/history/at` answers with a small page carrying the reason, because this frame is dark chrome and a bare line of error text rendered in it reads as a black rectangle, which is exactly how a deck that was merely too big to read looked for a release (#508: `git show` through `execFileSync` capped stdout at Node's 1 MB default and THREW `ENOBUFS` rather than truncating, and a blanket catch reported that as a missing revision). `GIT_MAX_BUFFER` (cli/git.mjs, 128 MB) is that cap named once and shared by every git read of a deck, a single-file deck is over 1 MB as a matter of course, and only a ref git itself does not know is a 404. `⏎` **asks** (it names the version, says the restore is written as a NEW commit and that nothing is rewritten), and `⏎` again does it. Restoring is recoverable, but recoverable is not the same as intended, and the whole premise of a history is that you opened it to look rather than to decide. Restoring used to be its own overlay with its own list and its own name for a commit; folding it in means there is one place a version is looked at and one place it is chosen, rather than two that had already begun to differ. It is the git-level sibling of `Z`/`⇧Z`: `Z` takes back a keystroke, this takes back a session. **In no-trust mode `H` opens the deck update instead** (`NO_TRUST#UPSTREAM`): a presented deck has no edit server and so no history to preview, and the live question there is whether the author has pushed anything since you cloned it.
- **Rehearsal timings** (`REHEARSAL_TIMINGS`): the speaker view's `⏱ rehearse timings` records how long each slide takes as you present, and `⏹ stop & save timings` writes the result onto the sections as `data-timing="<seconds>"` through the edit server (`POST /deck/edit/timings`, one edit for every slide, one undo entry), or, with no edit server, keeps it in this browser. From then on the speaker view shows a pace line: this slide's elapsed against its planned time (red past five seconds over) and the talk's total against the plan. The deck's own `data-timing` always wins over a browser's memory, because the file is the source of truth. The palette's **Rehearse timings…** opens the speaker view if it is not already open and starts the clock in one step: a recorder that could only be found by opening a popup first was a recorder most presenters never found.
- **Speaker view**: `⌥⏎` / `Alt+Enter` (PowerPoint's presenter-view chord, the same on every OS and no F-key) (or the palette's *Speaker view — second window*) opens a popup (synced via BroadcastChannel): current + next slide thumbnails, notes (with `⟨CLICK⟩` markers highlighted as the matching build lands), elapsed timer, build step list (provider labels). **Rehearse mode**: pressing `⌥⏎` again in the deck, or `S` in the popup (the header badge also toggles), swaps the prose notes for the slide's `aside.rehearse` cue cards (DECK_ANATOMY): rendered large and bold, one cue per segment, same said/now/next highlighting. Slides without rehearse notes fall back to the full prose. `S` in the deck only opens a new popup when none is connected; while one is open it toggles the mode. **Phone remote**: when the deck is being played by `decklight <deck> --no-trust --remote` (below), the speaker view also shows a QR of the controller URL: click it to enlarge for scanning across a lectern. It appears only when a remote is actually on, since without it there is no LAN URL to encode.
- **Phone remote** (`decklight <deck.html> --remote`, in either mode): the server also listens on the LAN and serves `GET /deck/remote`: a self-contained controller page with prev/next and a live "slide x/N" readout, no external asset of any kind (a phone is off-loopback, so anything it fetched from elsewhere would be a hole). A tap is relayed as a named `remote` event on the SSE stream the deck already holds, and the deck POSTs its position back, so the readout tracks the deck however it moved (phone, keyboard, or click). `POST /deck/remote/pos` is the deck→phones direction and **accepts only loopback callers**: the deck is the one thing that knows the position, it reports from this machine, and a phone, or anyone who obtained the QR token, posting a fabricated `{i,n}` would desync the readout every other phone shows. `--remote` widens the **listener** and nothing else: off this machine **only** `/deck/remote/*` answers, and only with the per-run random token the printed URL and its QR embed. The deck itself and every file beside it stay unreachable from the LAN either way. One exported classifier decides it, `allowRemote(req, token)`, and `NO_TRUST#REMOTE` is why the flag widens nothing else: a clicker must never mean running `/deck/edit/*` on the LAN during a talk you are not watching. The QR is generated server-side with no dependencies, and a deck that never uses a remote grows by zero bytes.
- **Print/PDF**: `?print` renders all slides sequentially, every build complete, terminal casts fully expanded, one slide per page (`@media print` CSS). No JS needed after layout. Background media (DECK_ANATOMY) prints as a still: no `<video>` element is ever created in print: the slide's `data-background-poster` renders as the background image instead. Two handout variants restructure the same fully-built render onto portrait pages (one `.print-page` per sheet): `?print=handout` groups slides three to a page, each scaled slide beside a column of ruled note-taking lines (page count = ⌈N/3⌉); `?print=notes` gives one page per slide with that slide's speaker notes rendered underneath. Slides without notes keep their page with an empty notes block. Both are palette rows too (**Print with notes**, **Print handout**), beside the plain print view, so a presenter reaches them without knowing the query string. **Page count equals slide count** (⌈N/3⌉ for handout): the page box has no margin, so the document has none either: the UA's default body margin would push the last slide's final pixels past the last page box and emit a blank final sheet. **`decklight pdf <deck.html>`** writes that render to a file without a print dialog: headless Chrome over the deck's own `?print`, one slide per 1280×720 page (960×540 pt), `-o` for the path, `--theme` to export in another theme (it rides `?theme=`), `--wait` for decks that need longer to settle. Slides the overflow guardrail flagged are named on stderr by slide number: the PDF is still written, because a clipped slide is worth knowing about, not worth refusing. No Chrome, or no file produced, exits non-zero rather than leaving a silent empty PDF. In write mode each of the three PDFs is a palette row of its own (**Export to PDF…**, **Export a PDF with notes…**, **Export a PDF handout…**), beside **Export to PowerPoint…** and named apart from the *print view* rows above, which open the same pages in a tab rather than writing a file anybody can be sent. Chrome is **awaited, not blocking** (`runAsync`): not for the deadlock reason `pptx` has, but because the edit server runs `pdfMain` in its own process for those rows, and a synchronous child would freeze live reload, the SSE stream and the page itself for as long as Chrome takes to print.
- **PowerPoint export**: **`decklight pptx <deck.html>`** writes a `.pptx` in which every slide is a picture (rendered at 1280×720 with its builds complete, through the deck's own served page so relative assets resolve) and every speaker note is a real note. It is **lossy by design** and `NON_GOALS` says why: the round-trip is not a goal, and the audience for the command is the people around a decklight user who ask for "the file". `-o` for the path, `--theme` to export in another, `--wait` per slide. **Hidden slides are not in the file** (`HIDDEN_SLIDES`), the same rule `pdf`, `video` and `voiceover` keep, and not merely as a preference: a deep link onto a hidden slide lands on its nearest shown neighbour, so exporting one wrote that neighbour's picture a second time under the hidden slide's notes. The numbering closes up in the exported file; a deck with nothing shown is a refusal rather than an empty one. The deck is served from the **current directory** when it lives under it (so a deck in a subdirectory still reaches the `dist/` and `themes/` at the top of its project) and from **its own directory** otherwise: a deck elsewhere is served, not refused. In write mode it is also a palette row, **Export to PowerPoint…**, because a presenter who needs the file needs it from where they are: the row asks the edit server (`POST /deck/edit/export`, `{ kind, theme | gen }`: `pptx`, `pdf`, `pdf-notes`, `pdf-handout`, `video`, `bundle`), which runs the same command in the same process: never an edit, so it takes no undo entry and leaves the deck byte-for-byte as it was. **Every export is in the theme on screen** (#547): the render is a fresh browser that cannot see the pick saved in this one, so the row names it (`theme`, a theme name and nothing else) and the server passes it on as `--theme`. A theme that lives only in this browser (a saved custom theme, an unsaved ⌃T roll) has no name a fresh render would know, so it goes as its **tokens** (`gen`: base64url JSON `{ name, tokens }`, at most 16 KB, the form the picker's previews already load) and the server passes that on as `--gen`, which each render tool puts on the deck URL as `?gen=`; the runtime vets every token before it becomes CSS (`safeTokens`). `video`, `pptx` and `pdf` take `--gen` from the command line too, and refuse it alongside `--theme`: one theme per render. **One export at a time**, whatever the kind (a second is refused, not queued): two exports at once is still two browsers on one machine, and the second would only be slower. A machine with no Chrome gets a sentence rather than a dead edit server, because the export must not take the session down with it. An export is tens of seconds, so the server **says where it is**: each slide is announced on the `export` channel of the same SSE stream the agent chip rides, and the deck rewrites its one progress row from it: one row that keeps telling the truth, never a toast per slide. **Bundle into one file…** is the row for the file most often sent: `decklight bundle`, run in the edit server, writing `<deck>-standalone.html` beside the deck: the CLI's own name, so the row and the command write the same file.
